Assistant, plugins, simulations, servers

afx.ask("plot the maximum stress over time", agent=True)
afx.plugins(); afx.load_plugin("tools.py")
afx.submit("lmp -in in.melt", host="rocm-server", workdir="~/runs", gpu="amd:0", outputs="dump.*", auto_load=True)
afx.sweep("lmp -var T {T} -in in.melt", T=[300, 600, 900], host="cluster")
afx.jobs(); afx.job(id); afx.cancel(id); afx.gpus("rocm-server"); afx.hosts()
afx.servers(); afx.load_remote("cluster", "/scratch/run.e")

Notebooks

auraFX runs Jupyter notebooks two ways:

  • The Notebook panel (Windows › Notebook): Markdown and Python cells run in the same Python as the console (Shift+Enter runs a cell and moves on, Run all), with their printed output, values, errors and pictures below them. Open… and Save read and write .ipynb files, so a notebook moves between this panel and Jupyter.
  • As a Jupyter kernel: aurafx --install-jupyter-kernel once, then choose auraFX (Python) as the kernel of a notebook in JupyterLab, Jupyter Notebook or an editor that runs notebooks. Jupyter starts auraFX with its window; the cells drive it through afx, and their output, values, errors and pictures come back to the notebook. Tab completes names; shutting the kernel down closes the window.

In either, afx.snapshot() shows a picture of the 3D view in the notebook (views="all": every view as laid out) and afx.display(x) shows a picture file, PNG bytes or any object as text.

w = afx.wavelet()
w.slice(normal=[0, 0, 1]).set(color_by="RTData")
afx.snapshot(width=640, height=400)

The Help › Python API cheat sheet window lists the same calls inside the app, and every function has a docstring (help(afx.lighting)).

AI assistant

The Assistant panel (right side; Windows › Assistant, or the Ask AI button) is a chat with a language model that knows the app and your data. Ask in plain words: “slice this through the middle and color by temperature”, “what arrays does this file have?”, “plot the maximum stress over time”, “why does my contour look cracked?”. The assistant answers and, when something should happen in the app, writes Python using the afx API.

What the assistant sees

A text description of your pipeline: the objects, their filters and parameters, array names with ranges, bounds, cell types and time steps, plus the last error. It never receives the data itself. The description is rebuilt for every message.

Running the code it writes

Code appears in a block with a Run code button; nothing runs until you press it, unless auto-run code is ticked. With agent mode on (the default), the assistant runs its code itself, looks at the result (values, errors, the pipeline) and keeps going until the task is done, so “find where the plate yields and plot it” becomes several steps without you in between.

afx.ask("make an isosurface at the mean temperature")
afx.ask("find where the plate yields and plot the max stress over time", agent=True)

Showing it the view. Tick the camera box next to Send and a picture of the 3D view goes with the message, for models that see images (Claude, GPT-4o, and Ollama models such as llava, gemma or qwen-vl): ask “what stands out in this picture?” or “is this mesh refined enough near the hole?”. From Python: afx.ask("describe the view", view=True).

Describe a filter. Filters › Describe a filter… (also in the command palette) takes a sentence about what you want to see in the selected object, such as “regions where the von Mises stress is above 300 MPa” or “the vortex cores of the flow”; the assistant adds the filters that show it (in agent mode, so it looks at the arrays and ranges first), and its steps appear in the Assistant panel.

Privacy. In the assistant’s settings: Local only sends nothing off your network (only an Ollama server at a local or private address, and no cloud models; anything else is refused with the reason), and Hide file paths replaces your home folder with ~ and full paths with file names in what is sent. The data itself is never sent, only the pipeline’s description (names, arrays, ranges) and the results of code the assistant runs.

A script can wait for the assistant and read what it said: afx.assistant_status() returns {"busy", "agent", "messages", "last", "error"} (an answer is arriving, agent mode is iterating, the number of messages, the last answer, the last error).

Modelling parts

The assistant knows the CAD modeler (see CAD modeling). Ask it to start a part (“a base plate 60 by 40 by 8 mm; name the dimensions width, depth and thickness”), to build it (“add a wall along the back edge, two 6 mm holes, round the corner”), and to change it (“make the plate 80 mm wide and the holes 8 mm”). It names dimensions, uses the part’s faces by name, checks the volume against numbers it worked out, and can open the part in the CAD environment, edit the working copy, and apply it; when applying would break a node below the part, it is asked for the fixes. It also places parts side by side (assemblies) and uses the face operations (patterns on a face, draft, mirror).

Prompts and skills

Prompts beside the model list offers saved prompts: one click puts it in the box, with {object} replaced by the selected object’s name (Describe, Find problems, Slice and color and Plot over time come with auraFX). Save the message as a prompt keeps what you typed. Edit, add or remove them in Settings › AI assistant › Prompts.

Skills are Markdown files in the skills folder beside the settings (~/.config/aurafx/skills on Linux; Open in Settings › AI assistant › Skills shows it): the text of every ticked one goes with each request, for house rules (“lengths are in micrometres”, “color temperatures with Inferno”), a lab’s usual steps or naming conventions. Untick a skill to leave it out.

Providers

Choose the provider and model in the panel’s drop-downs or in Settings:

ProviderSetup
Ollama (local or cloud models)the server URL from OLLAMA_HOST or Settings; the model list comes from the server
Anthropicset ANTHROPIC_API_KEY or paste a key in Settings
OpenAI-compatible (OpenAI, OpenRouter, Groq, vLLM, LM Studio…)the base URL and key in Settings

No model is chosen at first: the model list beside the provider shows <no model> until you pick one, and the choice is kept for the next start. With <no model>, messages are not sent: pick a model first.

Answers stream as they are produced. The suggestion buttons under the welcome text are good first questions for a new file.

Plugins

A plugin is a Python file that adds filters, sources and file readers. They appear in the Filters and Sources menus, in the command palette, in Python (node.my_filter(), afx.my_source()), in the Open dialog (readers) and to the AI assistant, like the built-in ones.

Where plugins live

  • The plugin folder: Edit › Plugins… › Open plugin folder (~/.config/aurafx/plugins on Linux, the auraFX config folder on macOS and Windows). Every .py file in it is loaded.
  • Folders listed in the AURAFX_PLUGIN_PATH environment variable.
  • Files or folders added with Add file or folder… in the Plugins window.
  • From a script: afx.load_plugin("path/to/tools.py").

Plugins reload when you save them, and the objects that use their filters re-run. Files whose name starts with _ are skipped (use them for shared helpers). A plugin that fails to load shows its error in the Plugins window and the Log; switch a plugin off with its checkbox. New plugin… writes a working example and opens it in your editor.

A filter

import aurafx as afx
import numpy as np

@afx.filter(category="Flow")
def speed(data, vectors=afx.ArrayParam(vector=True), name="speed"):
    """Magnitude of a vector array."""
    v = data.point_data[vectors]
    data.point_data[name] = np.linalg.norm(v, axis=1)
    return data

Keyword arguments become parameters in Properties; their type comes from the default: float, int, bool, str, a 3-tuple (a vector), afx.Choice([...]), afx.ArrayParam(vector=False, cells=False) (an array of the input) and afx.Code("...") (a text box). The docstring’s first line is the description. Other decorator options: label, description, keywords, id, and inputs=2 or inputs=(2, None) for filters with several inputs (the function receives a list).

data is an afx.Data, a copy of the input: data.points (N×3), data.point_data["T"], data.cell_data[...], data.field_data[...] (assign to add, del to remove), data.n_points, data.n_cells, data.bounds, data.type, data.cells (offsets, connectivity, VTK types), data.blocks(), data.copy(). The spellings data.PointData.append(a, "name") and data.CellData work too. Build new data with afx.make_points, afx.make_polydata, afx.make_image, afx.make_grid and afx.make_multiblock.

Sources and readers

@afx.source(category="Shapes")
def helix(turns=4.0, points=400):
    t = np.linspace(0, turns * 2 * np.pi, int(points))
    return afx.make_polydata(np.c_[np.cos(t), np.sin(t), t / 10], lines=[list(range(len(t)))])

@afx.reader(extensions=["xyzt"], label="XYZ + temperature")
def read_xyzt(path):
    a = np.loadtxt(path, ndmin=2)
    d = afx.make_points(a[:, :3])
    d.point_data["T"] = a[:, 3]
    return d

Readers take precedence over the built-in readers for their extensions.

Rules

  • Plugin functions run on a worker thread while the pipeline runs: do not call pipeline functions (afx.load, node.slice(), show…) inside them.
  • Ids must be unique and cannot replace built-in filters.
  • For one-off code, the Programmable Filter, Programmable Source and Python Calculator run Python stored in the pipeline (and in state files).

Panels

A plugin can add a small window of its own controls: @afx.panel("Title") on a function that gets a ui. It appears under Windows › Plugin panels. The function runs when the panel opens and again whenever a button is pressed or a value changes, so it reads like a script from top to bottom: ui.button(text) is True on the run its press causes, and ui.slider, ui.number, ui.checkbox, ui.text and ui.choice give their current values, which last between runs. ui.label(text), ui.separator() and ui.image(path) show things. An error in the function shows in the panel, under what it drew before.

import aurafx as afx

@afx.panel("Scale tools")
def scale_tools(ui):
    ui.label("Scale the selected object")
    f = ui.slider("factor", 2.0, 0.1, 10.0)
    axis = ui.choice("axes", ["all", "x", "y", "z"])
    if ui.button("Apply"):
        s = [f if axis in ("all", a) else 1 for a in "xyz"]
        afx.active().transform(scale=s)

Unlike filters, panel functions run on the Python thread between pipeline runs, so they may call afx.load, filters and show.

Rust plugins

Filters and sources can also be written in Rust, compiled into a shared library (.so on Linux, .dylib on macOS, .dll on Windows) and put in the plugin folder, or added with Edit › Plugins › Add file, or afx.load_plugin("libmy_filters.so"). The aurafx-plugin crate in the auraFX source gives the types and a macro:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
use aurafx_plugin::{export_plugin, DataSet, Filter, Param, Params};

fn scale_points(inputs: &[DataSet], p: &Params) -> Result<DataSet, String> {
    let mut d = inputs[0].clone();
    let f = p.f64("factor", 2.0);
    // ... change the points of d ...
    Ok(d)
}

export_plugin! {
    name: "My filters",
    filters: [Filter::new("scale_points", "Scale Points", scale_points)
        .describe("Scale the points about a centre.")
        .param(Param::float("factor", "Factor", 2.0).range(0.0, 100.0))],
}
}

with crate-type = ["cdylib"] in its Cargo.toml. Filter::source makes a source, .inputs(n) a filter of several inputs; Param::float, int, bool, string, vec3, choice and array are its settings in the Properties panel. crates/aurafx-plugin-example is a complete plugin (Scale Points and a Spiral source): cargo build --release -p aurafx-plugin-example builds it.

  • A plugin library is checked when it loads: it must be built against the same version of auraFX’s data crate (the message says which), since the data passes between them as bytes in that layout.
  • Errors returned by the plugin show as the filter’s error; a panic in the plugin is caught and shown the same way.
  • A library loads once per run: after rebuilding a plugin, restart auraFX.
  • Load only libraries you trust: a plugin runs with the same rights as auraFX.

Tutorials and examples

The examples/ folder of the repository holds small, complete workflows. The simulations are plain Python with NumPy that write a VTK file every few steps; run them from the Simulations panel (template Python pipeline, see Running simulations) or with afx.submit, and auraFX opens the files when the run ends. The tutorials are auraFX scripts: aurafx --script <file>, or paste them into the Python console.

FolderWhat it shows
examples/heat2d/2-D heat diffusion: the simplest Python-pipeline run; five output files are included, so it opens without running anything
examples/phase_field/spinodal decomposition (the Cahn-Hilliard equation, a spectral solver in NumPy): a phase-field time series in about ten seconds
examples/grain_growth/grain growth with the Potts model on a 2-D lattice: grain ids over Monte Carlo sweeps, for the grain filters
examples/polycrystal/geometry → hexahedral mesh → Voronoi grains → quality table → Exodus and .tex files → a crystal-plasticity case
examples/ebsd/an EBSD volume → grains → sizes, shapes, average orientations, IPF colors, pole figures, a CSV table

A simulation that writes a time series

Open the Simulations panel, choose Python pipeline, set the working directory to examples/phase_field and the input to cahn_hilliard.py, set Result files to ch_*.vtk and press Run. The log shows each step; when the run ends the series opens, colored by the concentration. Press play in the time toolbar, or

afx.submit("python3 -u cahn_hilliard.py", workdir="examples/phase_field", outputs="ch_*.vtk", auto_load=True)
afx.save_animation("spinodal.mp4", fps=12)

The grain-growth example works the same way (potts.py, result files grains_*.vtk); color it by grain_id with the Turbo color map. Segment Features (method Scalar, tolerance 0) on a step gives feature ids the grain filters measure (sizes, neighbors, shapes).

From a geometry to a solver case

examples/polycrystal/polycrystal_case.py builds a plate with a hole, meshes it with hexahedra, assigns 60 grains with one element block each, writes the mesh, the orientations and the input files of a crystal-plasticity case into polycrystal_out/. The chapters Geometry and meshing and Running simulations explain every step; with a solver installed, afx.run_case runs the case and opens the result.

An EBSD volume to statistics

examples/ebsd/ebsd_analysis.py segments the AM Bench window shipped in testdata/dream3d, cleans the grains, measures them, colors them by the inverse pole figure key, draws the pole figures in a second view and writes the grain table as grains.csv (Grains and crystals).

File formats

Every reader was checked against a reference implementation: the same points, cells, bounds and value ranges on the same files. The comparisons are listed in the verification notes (docs/reference/verification.md in the source tree).

Readers

FormatExtensionsNotes
Exodus II.e .exo .ex2 .g .genNetCDF-3 and NetCDF-4; blocks, side and node sets, nodal/element/global variables, time steps, displacements
VTK legacy.vtkASCII and binary, all dataset types
VTK XML.vtu .vtp .vti .vtr .vts .vtt, parallel .pvtu …, .vtm, .pvdinline, appended, base64; zlib and LZ4; curved (Lagrange/Bezier) cells
AMR (adaptive mesh refinement)VTK .vthb .vth, AMReX/BoxLib plotfiles (the folder, or its Header), FLASH and Chombo HDF5 files, Enzo (.hierarchy)levels of boxes with cell data; coarse cells under finer boxes are left out; see below
Hyper tree gridsVTK .htg (XML version 2)trees of refined cells; drawn as their leaves; see below
Cell grids (discontinuous Galerkin fields)VTK .dgcells whose fields are polynomials; drawn with the fields at the corners; see below
VTKHDF.vtkhdf .hdfimage data, unstructured grids, poly data, partitioned collections with their assembly, time steps; chunked and gzip storage; no HDF5 library needed
OpenFOAM.foamASCII/binary/gzip, decomposed and reconstructed cases, time directories, boundary patches
EnSight Gold.case .encasASCII and C binary, all element types, structured blocks, time sets, wildcard file names

NetCDF (CF).nc .nc4 .cdfclassic and NetCDF-4; lon/lat grids on a sphere with cell bounds, curvilinear grids with 2-D longitude/latitude (POP, CICE, ROMS), rectilinear otherwise; time steps; packing; fill values become NaN (grey); see below
CAM-SE.nc + connectivity file (found from the contents)CESM atmosphere on its cubed sphere: lon/lat map or sphere, surface, midpoint or interface variables, one level or all; see below

Tecplot.dat .plt .tec .tpASCII (ordered and FE zones, BLOCK/POINT, cell-centered variables) and binary .plt; .szplt is not read
PLOT3D.xyz .x .p3d .bin + .qlayout detected from the file (Fortran/C binary, byte order, precision, multi-grid, 2-D/3-D, IBLANK), ASCII

XYZ / extended XYZ.xyz .extxyzmulti-frame; properties and lattice from the comment line
PDB.pdb .entall atoms (hydrogens kept), MODELs as time steps, CONECT and distance bonds, residues and chains
Gaussian cube.cubethe density as a volume in Å, plus the atoms with bonds

GIS rastersGeoTIFF .tif, Esri ASCII grid .ascat their map coordinates, a cell per pixel, every band; no-data values become NaN (grey); see below
GIS vectorsShapefile .shp, GeoJSON .geojson .json, KML .kml, KMZ .kmz, GeoPackage .gpkg, FlatGeobuf .fgb, GML .gmlpoints, lines and polygon outlines (or filled areas) with their attributes as cell data, a block per layer; see below
EBSD maps and microstructuresTSL/EDAX .ang, Oxford .ctf, .h5ebsd stacks, EDAX and Bruker .h5, .dream3dcrystal orientations on a square grid with phases and quality columns; voxel volumes and meshes with feature and phase arrays; see Grains and crystals
SPPARKS sites.spparks, .sites; lattice text dumps (id i1 x y z)a site file without a Sites section (a lattice filling its box) and a dump of every site of a lattice open as a volume with FeatureIds (from i1); a site file with coordinates opens as points
Surfaces.stl .ply .obj, .offOFF also with point colors (COFF) and face colors
ScenesglTF 2.0 .gltf .glb, VRML 2.0 .wrltriangles, lines and points of every placed mesh (moved by its nodes or transforms), normals, vertex colors; a block per mesh; see below
Medical and microscopy volumesNRRD .nrrd .nhdr, MetaImage .mhd .mha, MRC .mrc .map .rec, DICOM .dcm (or the folder of a series), NIfTI .nii .nii.gz .hdr, OME-TIFF .ome.tifimage data at the file’s spacing, origin and orientation; see below
Point clouds (lidar)LAS .las, LAZ .laz, PTS .ptsLAS 1.0 to 1.4, point formats 0 to 10, with intensity, returns, classification, scan angle, GPS time and colors; see below

Nek5000.nek5000 (or a data file name0.f00001)spectral elements as hexahedra (quadrilaterals in 2-D), velocity, pressure, temperature, passive scalars, time steps
LS-DYNAd3plot and its family filessolids, shells, beams; states with moving nodes, velocities, accelerations, stresses, plastic strain, beam resultants; see below
Nastran bulk data.bdf .nas .bulk, .dat with GRID cardssmall, large and free fields; coordinate systems; solid, shell, bar and point elements with their properties as blocks
Fluent.msh .casASCII and binary sections; cells rebuilt from their faces; cell zones as blocks, boundary zones as side sets
CSV / TSV.csv .tsv .txtdelimiter and header detection; tables with x, y, z columns become points

Numbered files (run_0001.vtu, frame_0001.png, scan_0001.las…) open as a series when one of them is opened; a wildcard name (img_*.png) stacks pictures into one volume instead.

Medical and microscopy volumes

FormatNotes
NRRD (.nrrd, detached .nhdr + data)raw, gzip and ASCII encodings, any byte order; spacing from spacings or space directions (also the orientation), origin from space origin; a first axis of kind vector, RGB-color… gives components. The array is NRRDImage
MetaImage (.mha, .mhd + .raw/.zraw)every element type, zlib-compressed data, Offset, ElementSpacing, TransformMatrix, channels. The array is MetaImage
MRC / CCP4 (.mrc, .map, .rec, .st)cryo-EM maps and tomograms: modes 0 (int8), 1 (int16), 2 (float32), 6 (uint16), 12 (float16); the voxel size from the cell and sample counts; the origin; the axis order of the file (columns, rows, sections) put back to x, y, z
DICOM (.dcm, extension-less files, or a folder)a file opens its whole series (the files of its folder with the same series id), ordered along the slice normal; spacing from Pixel Spacing and the distance between slices, position and orientation from the patient coordinates; values rescaled by slope and intercept (Hounsfield units for CT). A folder opens its largest series. Compressed (JPEG) files are not read: save them uncompressed
NIfTI (.nii, .nii.gz, or a .hdr with its .img)NIfTI-1 and NIfTI-2, either byte order; the values as NIFTI, scaled by the header’s slope and intercept; a 4th (time) and 5th (vector) dimension become components; placed by the sform, else the qform, else the voxel sizes.
OME-TIFF (.ome.tif)the planes in the order the OME-XML gives (Z, channels, times), the physical pixel sizes, one array per channel named after it, times as time steps

Axes that a file swaps or reverses (rows of a DICOM picture running down, a NRRD space direction along −x) are laid out along +x, +y, +z, so the points sit where the file puts them. An oblique orientation (axes not along x, y and z) is kept as the image’s orientation, but auraFX places the points along the axes: measure in the grid’s own frame for such files.

ct = afx.load("scans/patient7")            # a folder of DICOM slices
mri = afx.load("brain.nrrd")
cells = afx.load("cells.ome.tif")          # DAPI, GFP... arrays; time steps

Point clouds

LAS and LAZ files (lidar) open as points with the arrays Intensity, ReturnNumber, NumberOfReturns, Classification (2 ground, 5 high vegetation, 6 building…), ScanAngle (degrees), UserData, PointSourceId, GpsTime and Color (drawn as its own colors), at real coordinates (scaled and offset). LAZ (compressed LAS) is read too. Point Gaussian or Eye-Dome Lighting (View › Lighting) suit large clouds; Threshold on Classification keeps one class.

PTS files (.pts, a count line then x y z intensity r g b per point, several scans one after another) open as points with Intensity, Colors and, for several scans, ScanId.

Scenes

glTF 2.0 (.gltf with embedded or separate buffers, .glb) and VRML 2.0 (.wrl, also gzipped) files from modelling programs and web viewers open as surfaces: every mesh of the scene, moved by its node’s (or transform’s) translation, rotation and scale, with its normals, vertex colors (Colors) and texture coordinates; a block per mesh, named after its node. VRML IndexedFaceSet, IndexedLineSet and PointSet are read, with per-vertex or per-face colors; PROTO definitions are not.

Particles on HDF5

  • H5Part files (Step#0, Step#1… groups) give one time step per group: points at x, y, z with every other dataset as a point array, times from each step’s TimeValue.
  • openPMD series (HDF5; one file holding all iterations, or one file per iteration, which opens with its siblings) give one time step per iteration at its time in seconds: the meshes as image data (grid spacing, offset and axis labels in metres, values times unitSI) and a block per particle species (positions plus position offsets, every record as a point array, constant records filled in).

LS-DYNA results

Open d3plot: its family files (d3plot01, d3plot02…) are read with it. The mesh holds the solids (tetrahedra, pyramids and wedges stored as hexahedra with repeated nodes are made the cells they are), thick shells, beams and shells, with Material, ElementId (the user numbers when the file has them) and ElementKind (1 solid, 2 thick shell, 3 beam, 4 shell). Each state moves the nodes to their current positions; Displacement is the move, with Velocity, Acceleration and Temperature when written, Stress (6 components) and EffectivePlasticStrain of solids and shells (the middle integration point), BeamResultants, and the global energies as field data. Fluid, adaptive and 8-node shell models are not read.

Solver meshes

Nastran bulk data (GRID, CTETRA, CPENTA, CHEXA, CPYRAM with their mid-side nodes, CTRIA3, CQUAD4, CBAR/CBEAM/CROD, CONM2; small, large and free fields with continuations; CORD2R, CORD2C and CORD2S coordinate systems) and Fluent meshes and cases (.msh, .cas; ASCII and binary node, cell and face sections; the cells are rebuilt from their faces, polyhedra kept as polyhedra) open as meshes with the auraFX block and set arrays: the Nastran property or the Fluent cell zone is the ElementBlock, Fluent boundary zones are side sets named after the zones. Both are also written (see Writers).

CGNS files come in two storage formats with the same .cgns extension: HDF5, the default since CGNS 3.4, and ADF, which older solvers and older CGNS libraries write. auraFX looks at the file and reads either one the same way, with File › Open or afx.load("wing.cgns"). Both the 64-bit ADF layout and the older 32-bit one are read, in either byte order. Links inside the file and links to other ADF files are followed. A link to an HDF5 file from an ADF file is not.

NetCDF climate and ocean files

Files that follow the CF conventions are placed by their coordinates:

  • Longitude and latitude axes put the grid on a sphere of radius 1. A third (vertical) axis becomes the radius. Depths marked positive="down" are turned around, and all levels are shifted so that none is below zero, so deeper levels lie further in (the deepest at the center).
  • Ocean and sea-ice models such as POP (CESM’s ocean), CICE and ROMS use index dimensions (nlat, nlon) and give each point’s longitude and latitude in 2-D variables (TLONG, TLAT) named in the coordinates attribute. These become curvilinear grids on the sphere. The coordinate variables themselves are not shown as data.
  • When the variables live on different grids (say TEMP on depth, latitude and longitude, and SSH on the surface only), Reader options › Variables on picks the grid. Python: ds.set(variables="(nlat, nlon)"). The grid with the most dimensions is read first.

CAM-SE output (the spectral-element core of CESM’s atmosphere model) comes as two files: the data, with columns ncol at lat/lon, and a connectivity file whose element_corners joins the columns into quads. Open the data file. auraFX finds the connectivity file in the same folder, or you can name it under Reader options › Connectivity file (Python: ds.set(connectivity="ne30np4_connectivity.nc")). It draws the grid on a flat lon/lat map:

  • Quads that cross the 0/360° seam are moved to the side where they stick out least, on copies of their points, so the map runs a little past 0 and 360.
  • Variables picks the surface variables [time, ncol], or those on midpoint (lev) or interface (ilev) levels. With All levels on, the default, each quad becomes a column of hexahedra, the top level highest, and the lev (or ilev) array holds each point’s level. With it off, Level chooses one level.
  • Layout › Sphere puts the columns on a sphere instead. There is no seam on a sphere.
  • A single level lies at height 0.

MPAS output (from the ocean, atmosphere or land-ice models of the Model for Prediction Across Scales) is NetCDF too. When a .nc file has MPAS’s mesh variables (xCell, cellsOnVertex), auraFX reads it as MPAS instead of as a CF grid:

  • Each MPAS cell center becomes a point, and the cells around each MPAS vertex are joined into a triangle (or a quad, on quad meshes). On a sphere, the points lie at the planet’s radius in meters.
  • Variables on cells (temperature, ssh…) become point data, and variables on vertices (relativeVorticity…) become cell data. Time steps are numbered 0, 1, 2…, and the date of each step (xtime) is kept with the data.
  • For other dimensions than time and position, the first index is used, except the vertical levels, which you choose with the reader options below.

Reader options

Some readers have settings for the file they read. They appear under Properties › Reader options when the file is selected, and from Python they are set like any other property, with node.set(...). Changing one reads the file again; the choice is saved with the state file. Reset to defaults goes back to the file as first opened.

An MPAS file offers:

OptionPythonWhat it does
Vertical levelvertical_level=2which level of 3-D variables is shown (0 is the top of the ocean, the bottom of the atmosphere)
All levels (multilayer)multilayer=Truestacks every level: each triangle becomes a column of wedges, and points carry each level’s values
Layer thicknesslayer_thickness=10000the distance between levels in the multilayer view, in meters on the sphere and degrees on the map; 0 picks one so that the whole stack is easy to see
Layers go up (atmosphere)atmosphere=Truestacks levels outwards (or upwards on the map) instead of downwards
Layoutlayout="Lat/Lon map""Sphere" (default) or a flat map with longitude and latitude in degrees
Map center longitudecenter_lon=0the longitude in the middle of the map (180 by default)
 
ocean = afx.load("output.nc")
ocean.set(vertical_level=10)                         # the 11th level down
ocean.set(layout="Lat/Lon map", multilayer=True)     # all levels, as a flat 3-D map

On the map, cells that cross its left or right edge are drawn on both sides, so the map reaches a little beyond 360°.

  • The triangles at a mesh boundary and, when the file has maxLevelCell, the triangles that touch land are left out, so land shows as holes. In the multilayer view, the levels below the sea floor are left out too, and each point carries its level’s value.
  • Reader options do not reach files opened on another machine through a server yet.

Verified against a reference implementation; the comparisons are listed in the verification notes (docs/reference/verification.md in the source tree).

GIS rasters

A GeoTIFF is a TIFF that also records where it lies on the map. auraFX notices these tags and reads the file as a map raster instead of as a picture. Esri ASCII grids (.asc), a common text format for elevation models, and ERDAS Imagine files (.img, common in remote sensing) are read the same way. Open them with File › Open or afx.load("dem.tif").

  • The raster becomes image data with one cell per pixel, placed at its map coordinates (meters for UTM, degrees for longitude/latitude), with north up.
  • Values are cell data. A single band, such as an elevation model, is named Elevation. In a file with several bands, the first is Elevation and the others are Band 2, Band 3… An RGB(A) image becomes one Elevation array with 3 or 4 components.
  • Pixels equal to the file’s no-data value become NaN and are drawn grey (after Warp By Scalar they leave a hole, since a NaN height has no place).
  • The field data holds NO_DATA_VALUE per band and MAP_PROJECTION. For a GeoTIFF that is the EPSG code (EPSG:32633). For an ASCII grid it is the text of the .prj file beside it.
  • 8- to 64-bit integer and floating-point bands are read, compressed or not, in strips or tiles, with the bands stored together or one after another. A pixel-is-point GeoTIFF is moved half a pixel to its corner, as GDAL does.
  • ERDAS Imagine: every layer of the same size is a band (1-, 2- and 4-bit, 8- to 32-bit integer and 32- and 64-bit floating-point layers, in tiles, plain or run-length compressed), placed by its map information as GDAL does. The no-data value is the layer’s “non-initialized” value. MAP_PROJECTION is the EPSG code for UTM and longitude/latitude on WGS 84, and the projection’s name otherwise. The layers are bands as above: an RGB .img gives Elevation, Band 2 and Band 3 (it does not say which layer is which color). Data kept in a separate .ige file (very large images) is not read.

To see an elevation model as terrain, move the values to the points with Cell Data to Point Data, then raise them with Warp By Scalar:

dem = afx.load("dem.tif")                    # or dem.img (ERDAS Imagine)
terrain = dem.cell_data_to_point_data().warp_by_scalar(array="Elevation", scale=1.0)
terrain.set(color_by="Elevation")

Verified against a reference implementation; the comparisons are listed in the verification notes (docs/reference/verification.md in the source tree).

Other GDAL raster formats (NetCDF through GDAL, JPEG 2000…) are not read yet.

GIS vectors

Maps of points, lines and areas, such as countries, roads, rivers, wells or survey sites, open like any other file:

FormatFiles
ESRI Shapefilename.shp with name.dbf (attributes) and name.prj (coordinate system) beside it; open the .shp
GeoJSON.geojson, or a .json file that holds GeoJSON
KML (Google Earth).kml; each folder is a layer
KMZ.kmz: a zipped KML (Google Earth’s usual download); the KML document inside is read
GeoPackage.gpkg; each feature table is a layer
FlatGeobuf.fgb: a fast binary format for large vector data; one layer
GML.gml (GML 2, 3 and 3.2, as GDAL and most web feature services write it); each feature type is a layer, typed by the .xsd schema beside the file when there is one
MapInfo tablename.tab with name.map (geometry), name.id (its index) and name.dat (attributes) beside it; open the .tab
MapInfo interchangename.mif (geometry, in text) with name.mid (attributes) beside it; open the .mif
Esri file geodatabasea name.gdb folder; open the folder (or any file in it). Each feature class and table is a layer

What you get:

  • The coordinates stay as stored: longitude and latitude in degrees for most GeoJSON and KML files, or meters for projected shapefiles. Heights (the third coordinate) are kept, so a file with heights in meters on a map in degrees looks very tall. Flatten it with Transform (scale z by 0) if that gets in the way.
  • Points become vertices, lines become polylines, and polygon rings become closed lines.
  • Each feature’s attributes become cell data on its cells: whole numbers as integers, decimals as doubles, and anything else (names, dates) as text. 64-bit integers stay numbers.
  • MAP_PROJECTION in the field data holds the coordinate system: the .prj text, the EPSG code of a GeoPackage, FlatGeobuf or GML layer, the coordinate system text of a file geodatabase layer, or EPSG:4326 (longitude/latitude) for GeoJSON and KML.
  • File geodatabases store coordinates on a fine grid and add Shape_Length and Shape_Area attributes to lines and areas; both come through as stored.
  • MapInfo tables store coordinates as whole numbers on a grid set by the file (a millionth of a degree for longitude and latitude). auraFX converts them as GDAL does, rounding to that grid. A MapInfo region’s rings are grouped into areas with holes: a .tab file says which rings are holes. In a .mif file, a ring inside a larger one is a hole, as GDAL decides it.
  • GML in a geographic coordinate system named the long way (urn:ogc:def:crs:EPSG::4326) stores latitude first. auraFX swaps it back to longitude, latitude, as GDAL does, so GML lines up with the other formats.

Reader options (Properties › Reader options, or node.set(...)):

OptionChoices
Layer (files with several layers)All or one layer by name
BlocksOne per layer (default: one block per layer, a row of attributes per feature), or One per feature: one block per feature, slow for thousands of features
PolygonsOutlines, or Filled: triangles covering each area, with holes left open, to color areas by an attribute
Feature idsadds the feature number as _vtkPedigreeIds
 

countries = afx.load("countries.shp")
countries.set(polygons="Filled")
countries.set(color_by="pop", log_scale=True)
roads = afx.load("world.gpkg")
roads.set(layer="roads")                      # one layer of a GeoPackage
sites = afx.load("sites.fgb")                 # FlatGeobuf
rivers = afx.load("hydro.gml")                # GML, a block per feature type
places = afx.load("trip.kmz")                 # Google Earth KMZ
parcels = afx.load("parcels.tab")             # MapInfo (or parcels.mif)
lots = afx.load("city.gdb")                   # an Esri file geodatabase folder
lots.set(layer="lots", polygons="Filled")

A feature without geometry gives an empty block. Any layer of a file can be picked on its own, and by default all layers are read.

Verified against a reference implementation; the comparisons are listed in the verification notes (docs/reference/verification.md in the source tree).

Not read yet: curved geometry (arcs; a file geodatabase’s curves are read as straight lines between their vertices) and file geodatabase rasters and multipatches. In MapInfo files, text, arcs, rectangles, ellipses and collections are read without their geometry (their attributes are kept). Shapefile multipatches (3-D surfaces) are skipped.

AMR data (adaptive mesh refinement)

AMR simulations (AMReX, BoxLib, Castro, MAESTRO, Nyx, WarpX, FLASH, Enzo…) store their domain as levels of boxes: a coarse level everywhere and finer boxes where more detail was needed. auraFX reads:

  • AMReX / BoxLib plotfiles: a folder like plt00100 with a Header file. Open the Header file in File › Open (choose “All files”), drop the folder on the window, or afx.load("plt00100").
  • VTK AMR files (.vthb, .vth).
  • FLASH plot and checkpoint files (name_hdf5_plt_cnt_0007, name_hdf5_chk_0003; HDF5, FLASH 2 to 4). They usually have no extension: open them in File › Open with “All files”; auraFX recognizes them by their contents. Every block of the PARAMESH tree becomes a box on its refinement level (FLASH’s level 1 is auraFX’s Level 0), with every variable in unknown names as cell data in double precision. 1-D, 2-D and 3-D files are read.
  • Enzo output: open the .hierarchy file of a data dump (DD0010/data0010.hierarchy). The parameter file (data0010) and the grid files (data0010.cpu0000…) must be beside it. Each grid is a box on its level; fields keep their stored type (usually single precision). Reader option Convert to CGS multiplies each field by its #DataCGSConversionFactor from the parameter file. It is off by default.
  • Particles in FLASH and Enzo files: when a file has them, the reader option Data switches between Grid (the AMR data) and Particles, a point for every particle with its properties as point data (FLASH’s as doubles, Enzo’s in their stored types). Open the file twice to see both, as in the picture below. Chombo files with a particles group get the same option (properties as doubles).
  • Chombo files (.hdf5, .h5; 1-D to 3-D), as Chombo-based codes write them (level_0, level_1… groups with boxes). The component names become cell arrays, or point arrays in node-centered files. Ghost cells stored around each box are dropped, and the boxes are placed from prob_lo and the level’s dx (or vec_dx).
  • 4-D Chombo files (two space axes and two velocity axes, as gyrokinetic codes such as COGENT write them, single level): a 2-D grid over the space axes, whose every cell holds each component as an array with one value per velocity cell (parallel velocity major, magnetic moment minor). _vpar and _mu hold those cells’ velocity coordinates, and <component>_sum the sum over the velocity cells (leaving out velocity cells no box covers, which are NaN in the arrays). Color by a component of the array to see one velocity cell, or by the sum.

What you get:

  • One block per level (Level 0, Level 1, …), each holding that level’s boxes as image data with the variables as cell data. The Information panel lists them.
  • Coarse cells that a finer box covers are marked as hidden in vtkGhostType. Surfaces, slices, clips, contours, thresholds, Integrate Variables, Resample To Image and probing skip them, so every place is shown and counted once, at its finest level.
  • AMReX vector variables written as amrexvec_<name>_x, _y, _z become one vector <name>. In 2-D files the missing z is 0.
  • The time of the plotfile or dump is in the field data (TimeValue). Single- and double-precision files are read.
  • Outline draws the outline of every box, which shows where the refinement is.
  • Contours of cell data are stitched across levels (see below).

amr = afx.load("plt00100")                 # the plotfile folder
amr.set(representation="Outline")          # where the refinement is
s = amr.slice(normal=(0, 0, 1))            # finest data on the cut
s.set(color_by="density")
lev = amr.overlapping_amr_level_ids()      # LevelIdScalars: each cell's level
flash = afx.load("sedov_hdf5_plt_cnt_0010")  # FLASH
enzo = afx.load("DD0010/data0010.hierarchy")  # Enzo
enzo.set(cgs=True)                         # fields in CGS units
chombo = afx.load("plot.0010.2d.hdf5")      # Chombo
stars = afx.load("DD0010/data0010.hierarchy")
stars.set(data="Particles")                # the particles instead of the grid

Sources › AMR Gaussian Pulse makes a small AMR test dataset: a coarse box and two finer boxes holding a Gaussian pulse, in 2-D or 3-D. Filters › Overlapping AMR Level Ids adds the cell array LevelIdScalars with each cell’s level.

Filters › Resample AMR samples AMR data on one uniform grid, so tools that want a single image can use it (volume rendering, image filters, export). Number of samples sets the grid points per axis; with Whole domain off, Min and Max pick a box (clipped to the data, dropping the samples that fall outside). Each point takes the values of the finest cell holding it. With Transfer to nodes off, the values are taken at the cell centers instead and become cell data. Points no box holds are marked hidden.

grid = amr.resample_amr(samples=(64, 64, 64))
part = amr.resample_amr(samples=(32, 32, 16), whole_domain=False, min=(0, 0, 0), max=(1, 1, 0.5))

Resample AMR always uses every level of the data. 2-D FLASH files are read like 3-D ones. Ghost cells stored in Chombo files are dropped on every level. FLASH particles are given once each, with their own properties.

Verified against a reference implementation; the comparisons are listed in the verification notes (docs/reference/verification.md in the source tree).

Contours of AMR data

Values of AMR data sit at cell centers, and cells change size where levels meet. Averaging them to points box by box leaves gaps and overlaps along every level boundary. Filters › AMR Contour joins the cell centers of all levels into one mesh, the dual grid. Where a finer box meets coarser cells, its border cells reach out to the coarse cell centers, so the isosurface continues across the boundary without a crack. The other cell arrays are interpolated onto the surface. Contour on a cell array of AMR data does the same automatically. In 2-D data the result is closed isolines.

c = amr.amr_contour(array="density", values=[0.3])
c = amr.contour(array="density", values=[0.3])     # the same, for AMR cell data
dual = amr.amr_dual_grid()                          # cell values on one stitched mesh
cut = dual.clip(type="Scalar", array="density", value=0.3)   # a crack-free "dual clip"

Filters › AMR Dual Grid gives that mesh itself: one point per cell center, carrying all the cell arrays as point data. Clip, Slice, Stream Tracer and other filters that interpolate point data work on it across levels without seams.

On a test plotfile whose density isosurface is a sphere crossing from the fine box into the coarse ones, the contour is closed (every edge shared by exactly two triangles, the topology of a sphere), its area is within 0.2 % of the sphere’s, and its center is within 0.001 of the sphere’s center. The box-by-box contour had 346 open edges there. More in the verification notes (docs/reference/verification.md in the source tree).

Not read yet: plotfiles without FAB headers (the “NoFabHeader” variants) and AMReX particles. Not available yet: material-interface filters for CTH data (AMR Fragments, Material Interface).

Hyper tree grids

A hyper tree grid (HTG) starts from a grid of root cells. Each root cell is the top of a tree: a refined cell splits into 2×2×2 children (3×3×3 with branch factor 3; 2×2 or 3×3 in 2-D), and so on down to the leaves. Every cell of every tree, coarse or leaf, can hold values. A mask can hide cells. It is VTK’s tree-based format for adaptive meshes. auraFX reads VTK’s .htg files (XML version 2).

What you get:

  • The grid is drawn as its visible leaves. Every filter that works on cells (Slice, Clip, Threshold, Contour after Cell Data to Point Data, Cell Centers, Integrate Variables…) sees those leaves as voxels (pixels in 2-D) with their values.
  • Information shows the dimension, branch factor, number of visible leaves and depth. The cell count is the number of all cells (coarse ones included).
  • A grid with material interfaces is drawn cut to its material (see below). Other filters still see whole leaves.
  • Saving as .htg keeps the grid: its trees, the mask and every cell’s values (the version 2 files). Saving as any other format (.vtu and the rest) writes the visible leaves as an unstructured grid.

Sources:

  • Hyper Tree Grid builds a grid from a text descriptor. Levels are separated by |. Each level lists R (refined) or . (leaf) for every cell of that level, all trees in order; spaces are ignored. Dimensions (points per axis; 1 makes an axis flat), Branch factor, Max depth, Grid scale, and Use mask with a Mask string of the same layout (0 hides a leaf, 1 keeps it). The mask is ignored on refined cells.
  • Random Hyper Tree Grid refines cells at random. Split fraction is each cell’s chance of being refined, down to Max depth. The same Seed gives the same grid. Masked fraction hides about that share of the volume, whole subtrees at a time (the same settings hide the same cells). Its masking can hide a cell while leaving some cells below it unmasked; those stay hidden.

Filters (Filters › Data):

FilterWhat it does
Hyper Tree Grid To Unstructured Gridthe visible leaves as an unstructured grid, with OriginalIds (each leaf’s cell index)
Hyper Tree Grid Depth Limitercuts every tree at a depth: cells at that depth become leaves (Depth 0 keeps only the root cells)
Hyper Tree Grid Generate FieldsCellSize (volume, area or length of every cell), ValidCell (1 on visible leaves), CellCenter, and TotalVisibleVolume in the field data
Hyper Tree Grid To Dual Grida point at the center of every visible leaf, with the leaf’s values as point data, and a cell (voxel, pixel or line) joining the leaves around every corner. Use it for smooth contours and for filters that need point data. Points on the grid’s boundary sit on the boundary; next to masked leaves they move onto the face shared with the masked cell
Hyper Tree Grid Geometrythe outside of the visible leaves as polygons with their values: in 3-D the faces on the grid’s boundary and the walls next to masked cells (a face of a masked cell next to a coarser visible leaf carries that leaf’s values); in 2-D a quad per visible leaf, in 1-D a line
With material interfaces, mixed cells are cut to their material (see below). Fill material (on by default): off draws only the interface lines in 2-D and leaves out pure cells in 1-D and 2-D
Hyper Tree Grid Feature Edgesthe edges of the visible region as lines with their leaves’ values: the outline in 2-D, the outer corners and folds in 3-D
Hyper Tree Grid Evaluate Coarsefills the values of refined cells from their children: Min, Max, Sum, Average, Unmasked Average, Elder Child (the first child) or Splatting Average

Material interfaces. A grid can describe where a material ends inside some cells, for simulations that track interfaces between materials. The file names two cell arrays (VTK’s InterfaceNormalsName and InterfaceInterceptsName). For each cell they hold a normal n (three values) and intercepts (d1, d2, type); each interface is the plane n·x + d = 0:

  • type −1: one plane (d1); the material is where n·x + d1 ≥ 0;
  • type 1: one plane (d2); the material is where n·x + d2 ≤ 0;
  • type 0: the material lies between the two planes;
  • type 2 or more, or a zero normal: the cell is pure (all material).

Hyper Tree Grid Geometry (and the default drawing) cuts each mixed cell by its planes:

  • In 1-D and 2-D, each mixed leaf becomes the part of its line or quad that is in the material.
  • In 3-D, the faces of mixed cells and of their neighbors are drawn cut to the material, and each mixed cell adds its interface polygons.

With Fill material off, 2-D grids give only the interface lines. Saving as .htg keeps the interface arrays.

h = afx.random_hyper_tree_grid(dimensions=(7, 5, 1), max_depth=5, split_fraction=0.45, seed=3)
h.set(color_by="Depth", representation="Surface With Edges")
t = afx.hyper_tree_grid(dimensions=(3, 3, 1), max_depth=3, descriptor="R... | .R.. | ....")
coarse = h.hyper_tree_grid_evaluate_coarse(operator="Max")
top = h.hyper_tree_grid_depth_limiter(depth=2)
leaves = afx.load("tree.htg").hyper_tree_grid_to_unstructured_grid()
dual = h.hyper_tree_grid_to_dual_grid()
holes = afx.random_hyper_tree_grid(seed=3, masked_fraction=0.25)
walls = holes.hyper_tree_grid_geometry()
edges = holes.hyper_tree_grid_feature_edges()
h.save("tree.htg")
m = afx.load("interface_plane.htg")       # drawn cut to its material
lines = m.hyper_tree_grid_geometry(fill_material=False)

To Unstructured Grid gives every leaf its index in OriginalIds, masked files included. To Dual Grid makes points for visible leaves only. Cell Centers works on hyper tree grids directly; there is no separate Axis Reflection filter for them. .htg files auraFX writes read back unchanged.

Verified against a reference implementation; the comparisons are listed in the verification notes (docs/reference/verification.md in the source tree).

Not available yet: version 1 .htg files. Ghost Cells only matters for data split across processes. Interfaces are used by Geometry only: slicing or clipping a grid with interfaces works on whole leaves.

Cell grids

A cell grid holds cells whose fields are polynomials rather than one value per point or cell. Simulation codes using finite elements of the discontinuous Galerkin (DG) kind write fields this way. On each cell a field is a sum of basis functions, each times a coefficient stored in the file. A field can jump from one cell to the next. It is VTK’s newest data type. auraFX reads VTK’s .dg files (JSON).

Cells can be hexahedra, tetrahedra, wedges, pyramids, quadrilaterals, triangles, edges and vertices. Fields can be:

  • nodal (values at the corners, linear or quadratic; quadratic cells also have nodes on their edges, faces and middle), either shared between cells or kept per cell;
  • edge fields (the H(curl) kind, for electric fields: their tangential part is continuous across faces);
  • face fields (the H(div) kind, for fluxes: their normal part is continuous across faces);
  • constant fields, one value per cell.

Edge and face fields are turned from the cell’s own coordinates into space with the cell’s shape (its Jacobian), as in VTK. The cells’ shape can itself be quadratic (curved).

What you get:

  • The grid is drawn as its cells with every field evaluated at the corners. Quadratic cells are drawn as linear cells through their corners. Where cells meet, a field that jumps between them is averaged. Color by, the Spreadsheet, picking and every other filter see that mesh.
  • Information names the cell types and the number of fields (and, after Compute Sides, the number of sides shown).
  • Saving as .dg keeps the cell grid (every array, attribute and cell type, in the layout VTK writes). Saving as any other format writes the corner mesh as an unstructured grid.

Source (Sources › Cell Grid): one cell of a chosen Cell type with test fields: hgrad (1, 0, 0 at the first corner, 0 elsewhere), and on hexahedra, tetrahedra, wedges, quadrilaterals and triangles hcurl and hdiv (an edge and a face field whose first coefficient is 1). A vertex gets a constant field.

Filters (Filters › Data):

FilterWhat it does
Cell Grid To Unstructured Gridthe cells with the fields at their corners (what is drawn); corners at the same place become one point
Cell Grid Centersa point at the center of every cell with every field evaluated there (exact, not averaged)
Cell Grid Elevationadds a field: the distance from Origin along Axis (or, with Spherical distance, from Origin)
Cell Grid Transformmoves, rotates (degrees) and scales the cells, as Transform does, or another 3-D attribute shared at points or stored per cell. Edge and face fields follow the new shape
Cell Grid Warpmoves the cells’ points by Scale times a vector attribute shared at the points (the same basis and order as the shape)
Cell Grid Probefinds the cells holding the points of a second dataset (the Probe points): a point for every cell each one is in, with ContainingCellID (the cell’s number within its type), CellType (the VTK cell type number), ParametricCoordinates (where in the cell, in the cell’s own coordinates) and, when Attribute names a field, the field’s exact value there. Points in no cell are left out. A point on a face shared by two cells is listed twice
Cell Grid Compute Sidesfinds the sides of the cells and draws them instead of the 3-D cells: by default the faces on the outside (those not shared by two cells). Sides to include picks faces, edges or corners; see below
Convert To Cell Gridmakes a cell grid from a mesh of vertices, lines, triangles, quads, tetrahedra, hexahedra, wedges and pyramids (other cells are skipped): point data becomes fields shared at the points, cell data constant fields (renamed “(cell)” when point data has the same name)
 

g = afx.load("dgHexahedra.dg")
g.set(color_by="scalar2", representation="Surface With Edges")
centers = g.cell_grid_centers()
centers.set(color_by="curl1", points_as_spheres=True)
height = g.cell_grid_elevation(axis=(0, 0, 1))
cell = afx.cell_grid(cell_type="Tetrahedron")
mesh = g.cell_grid_to_unstructured_grid()
turned = g.cell_grid_transform(rotate=(0, 0, 30), scale=(1, 1, 2))
grid = afx.load("mesh.vtu").convert_to_cell_grid()
grid.save("mesh.dg")
cloud = afx.point_source(center=(1, 0.6, 0.5), radius=0.75, count=4000)
found = g.cell_grid_probe(cloud, attribute="quadratic")
outside = g.cell_grid_compute_sides()
edges = g.cell_grid_compute_sides(include="Edges of inputs", omit_renderable=False)

Cell Grid Probe looks up points in the cells. Select the cell grid, Ctrl+click the dataset whose points you want to look up (any dataset: its points are used), then choose Filters › Data › Cell Grid Probe. In Properties, Probe points picks the second input and Attribute the field to sample (empty: only find the cells). A cell is searched with a quick test first: the point must lie behind each of the cell’s faces, taken as flat planes through the corners (on a flat cell, the edges’ directions stand in for the normals). Then the point’s coordinates in the cell are found step by step from the cell’s middle. On a cell with curved faces a point just outside can pass the quick test; it is kept, with empty (NaN) coordinates and values. Points on an edge cell are kept when they lie between the two end planes and within the sphere around the edge, and are then kept only if they lie on the edge. The output points are in single precision, and with several cell types they are listed in the file’s order. The grey points in the picture are such points, next to the curved face the two cells share.

Cell Grid Compute Sides finds the sides of the cells. Every side of every cell is listed, by cell and then by side, and a side is matched with the same side of a neighbor cell whichever way round its corners go. A side is kept once, for the first cell that has it. Properties:

  • Sides to include:
    • Next lowest dimension (the default): the faces of 3-D cells, the edges of 2-D cells and the end points of edges;
    • Surfaces of inputs: the faces of 3-D cells;
    • Edges of inputs: the edges of 3-D and 2-D cells;
    • Vertices of inputs: the corners of every cell;
    • All sides: all of these.
  • Omit sides of surfaces and lines (on by default): 2-D and 1-D cells give no sides, since they can be drawn as they are.
  • Keep surfaces and lines (on by default): keep drawing the 2-D and 1-D cells and the sides already listed. 3-D cells are always hidden behind their sides.
  • Keep (advanced):
    • Boundary (the default): faces of 3-D cells (or edges of 2-D cells, end points of edges) found once, so not shared by two cells, and every lower side (edges and corners of 3-D cells) once;
    • Odd count: every side found an odd number of times;
    • Every side: each side once.

The sides are stored in the grid as lists of (cell, side number) pairs, one per side shape (“quadrilateral sides of vtkDGHex”), as VTK stores them. The fields on a side are the fields of its cell, so a field that jumps between cells shows the value from the cell the side belongs to. Running Compute Sides on its result adds the sides of those sides, for example the edges of the outer faces. Saving as .dg keeps the lists. Any filter other than the cell grid ones works on what is drawn: the sides. VTK’s markings of ghost and hidden points (which skip sides) are not read.

On quadrilaterals and triangles the cell’s shape matrix (its Jacobian) is flat, so it is completed with the cell’s unit normal; edge and face fields then lie in the cell’s plane, and Cell Grid Probe gives coordinates on flat cells and edges.

Verified against a reference implementation; the comparisons are listed in the verification notes (docs/reference/verification.md in the source tree).

Not available yet: cell grid range and copy filters. Slicing or clipping works on the corner mesh, so curved cells come out straight.

Writers

FormatExtensionsNotes
VTK XML.vtu .vtp .vti .vtr .vts .vtmappended binary; multiblocks as a folder of pieces
VTK hyper tree grid.htgXML version 2: the trees, mask and cell data
VTK cell grid.dgthe JSON layout VTK writes: arrays, attributes and cell types
VTK legacy.vtk
VTKHDF.vtkhdfall dataset kinds; multiblocks as a collection; time series with all_times=True; written with auraFX’s own HDF5 encoder (read by h5py and VTK)
Exodus II.exoblocks, nodal and element variables, every time step with all_times=True; HEX20 and HEX27 elements; the element blocks, side sets and node sets of generated meshes (see Geometry and meshing)
Abaqus input.inpnodes, elements (C3D8, C3D20, C3D27, tetrahedra, wedges, pyramids) in one element set per block, node sets, side sets as element-based surfaces
Gmsh.mshversion 4.1 ASCII: one physical volume per element block, one physical surface of quadrilaterals per side set (node sets are not part of the format)
Fluent.cas (or name.fluent.msh)ASCII: nodes, a cell zone per block, interior faces, a boundary zone per side set and the other outer faces in wall, zone names; 2-D or 3-D cells of one dimension
Nastran bulk data.bdf .nasfree field with full precision: GRID, CHEXA/CTETRA/CPENTA/CPYRAM (with mid-side nodes)/CTRIA3/CQUAD4/CBAR, a PSOLID/PSHELL/PBAR per block (named in a comment), node sets as SPC1 cards
MetaImage.mha .mhdimage data’s active array (point data, else cell data): all number types, channels, origin, spacing, orientation
Image stacks.png, .tifa volume as one 8- or 16-bit PNG per slice (name_0000.png…; other values scaled to 16 bits), or one multi-page TIFF (also 32-bit floats)
Parallel VTK.pvtu .pvtp .pvti .pvts .pvtrthe header and a folder of piece files: the blocks of a partitioned dataset, or 4 pieces cut from one dataset (afx.save takes pieces=)
Time series collection.pvdwith all_times=True, one VTK XML file per time step in a folder and the .pvd listing them with their times
EnSight Gold.caseC binary; one part per block (image, rectilinear and structured grids as blocks, the rest as elements of every standard type, polygons and polyhedra); every time step with all_times=True, the geometry written once when it does not change
CGNS.cgnsHDF5 storage (CGNS 4): a zone per block, image/rectilinear/structured grids as structured zones, the rest as one element section (MIXED when the cell types differ) plus NGON/NFACE for polygons and polyhedra; vectors as NameX/NameY/NameZ; with all_times=True one solution per step (and coordinates per step when the points move), but the cells must stay the same
Xdmf 3.xmf + .h5the description in the .xmf, the arrays in an HDF5 file beside it; block trees as spatial collections, time steps as a temporal collection that shares unchanged points and cells
Grain orientations.texfour header lines (the fourth B N), then one line per grain id from 1: φ1 Φ φ2 in degrees and a weight of 1 (meshes with grains from Assign Grains or Overlay Mesh); grains without elements get 0 0 0
Microstructure files.dream3dvolumes (an image geometry), point sets and meshes of one element type (vertex, edge, triangle, quad, tetrahedral, hexahedral geometries) with their cell, vertex, feature and phase arrays; multiblocks as one data container per block; the pipeline that made the data is recorded in the file (see Grains and crystals)
SPPARKS sites.spparksthe FeatureIds of a volume as the Values of a simple-cubic lattice filling the box, x fastest
CSV, STL, PLYa multiblock of surfaces saved as name.stl writes one file per block, name_<block>.stl (Feature Surfaces gives one per grain)
Surfaces for other programs.obj .glb .geoFile › Save Data of a surface: Wavefront OBJ (points, normals from a Normals array, faces, lines, points), binary glTF 2.0 (positions, normals, an 8-bit color array, triangles, lines and points) and Houdini text geometry (every point array of 1 to 4 numbers as a point attribute; polygons closed, lines open). These hold the data itself; Export Scene writes what is drawn, with its colors
Scenes.glb .obj .x3d .wrlsee Exporting
Pictures.png .svg, GIF/MP4/WebM animationssee Exporting

Files written by the VTK XML, VTKHDF, Exodus, EnSight, Xdmf and CGNS writers were read back with the same points, cells and values (verified against a reference implementation; the comparisons are listed in the verification notes, docs/reference/verification.md in the source tree). EnSight groups the cells of each part by type, and CGNS puts polyhedra after the other cells, so cells can come back in a different order (each keeps its values). Cell types a format does not have (Lagrange and Bezier cells, for example) are written by their corner points. A CGNS zone holds cells of one dimension: in a volume mesh, boundary faces and lines are left out.

Curved cells

VTK’s Lagrange and Bezier cells of any order, quadratic cells and serendipity cells are read with their node numbering, drawn curved and cut by the filters along their curvature; see Volumes, point clouds and curved cells.

Your own formats

A plugin reader is a Python function with @afx.reader(extensions=[...]); it takes precedence over the built-in reader for its extensions and appears in the Open dialog.

Exporting pictures, scenes and animations

Screenshots

File › Save Screenshot… (Ctrl+Shift+S) opens a small dialog:

  • Size: the view’s size, a multiple of it (2×, 4×), 1920×1080, 3840×2160, or any size; keep the view’s shape ties width and height together. Point sizes and line widths are in pixels, so they look thinner in a larger picture.
  • Background › transparent saves a PNG with an alpha channel, with clean edges.
  • Annotations includes the legend, axes-grid labels, time label, text annotations and the orientation axes, laid out as on screen and scaled with the picture.
  • PNG or SVG: an SVG holds the surfaces as shaded polygons sorted back to front, the lines and points, and the annotations as real text, for figures that scale without pixels (where surfaces cut through each other the boundary is jagged; convert to PDF with Inkscape if needed).

With ray tracing on, the picture is traced at the requested size first.

afx.screenshot("fig.png")                               # the view as shown
afx.screenshot("fig.png", width=3840, transparent=True) # keeps the view's shape
afx.screenshot("fig.png", width=1600, height=900, annotations=False)
afx.screenshot("fig.svg")

afx.screenshot returns only when the file is written, so a script can change the camera or the data between pictures.

All views in one picture

For a slide or a report with several views side by side, choose Picture of › all views in the same dialog: every visible 3D view and chart view is saved in one PNG, each where it sits in the window, with tab bars, panels and menus left out and the charts’ controls hidden (keep the chart views’ controls keeps them). chart views saves only the charts. The background between the views is the window’s, or transparent. The picture has the window’s pixels, so a larger (or full-screen) window gives a larger picture. Only views whose tab is in front are in the picture.

afx.screenshot("slide.png", views="all")            # every visible view, as laid out
afx.screenshot("charts.png", views="charts")
afx.screenshot("chart2.png", views="chart 2")       # one chart view ("chart" is the Charts panel)
afx.screenshot("view2.png", views="view 2", transparent=True)

Scenes for other programs

File › Export Scene… (afx.export_scene("scene.glb")) writes the visible objects, with their colors, as:

FormatNotes
glTF binary (.glb)for Blender, three.js, PowerPoint, Windows 3D Viewer; vertex colors, normals, translucency, materials (metallic, roughness)
OBJ (.obj)with vertex colors after the coordinates (Blender and MeshLab read them)
X3D (.x3d), VRML (.wrl)absolute coordinates, vertex colors

Volumes are not exported (they are not geometry).

Animations

File › Animation… records the time steps, a turntable or the keyframes as GIF, MP4 or WebM (VP9, for web pages; both need ffmpeg on the PATH) or a folder of PNG frames, of the active 3D view or of all views as laid out (afx.save_animation("all.mp4", views="all")); see Time, charts and animation.

Saving data

Right-click an object › Save data…, or node.save("out.vtu"), writes its output; the format follows the extension:

ExtensionFormat
.vtu .vtp .vti .vtr .vts .vtmVTK XML (appended binary); .vtm writes a folder of pieces
.vtklegacy VTK
.vtkhdfVTKHDF (HDF5); multiblocks as a collection with the block tree; all_times=True writes every time step into one file
.exoExodus II with blocks and variables; all_times=True writes every time step
.caseEnSight Gold (C binary): the case file plus a geometry file and one file per variable next to it; all_times=True writes every time step
.xmfXdmf 3: the description plus an .h5 file with the arrays; all_times=True writes every time step
.cgnsCGNS (HDF5); all_times=True writes every time step when the cells stay the same (moving points are fine)
.csvthe point data or the table
.stl .plysurfaces
 
d.save("slice.vtp")
d.save("run_all.exo", all_times=True)
d.save("run_all.vtkhdf", all_times=True)
d.save("run.case", all_times=True)      # run.case, run.geo, run.Temp0000, ...
d.save("run.xmf", all_times=True)       # run.xmf and run.h5
d.save("run.cgns", all_times=True)

When the data has several time steps and the chosen format holds them all (Exodus, VTKHDF, EnSight, Xdmf, CGNS), the Save dialog writes every step.

Running simulations

The Simulations panel (right side; Simulate › Run a simulation…, or Windows › Simulations) launches solvers and opens their results when they finish.

A new simulation

FieldMeaning
TemplateLAMMPS, MOOSE app, MOOSE polycrystal, SPPARKS grain growth, PINNACLE crystal plasticity, DAMASK, FFT texture file, LAMMPS polycrystal, OpenFOAM, Python pipeline or Custom command; fills in the program, input and result-file patterns (the polycrystal and grain growth templates also write their input files, see below)
Run onthis computer, or a remote machine from the SSH list; via Slurm submits with sbatch there (partition, time and extra #SBATCH lines appear)
Name, Working directorywhere the run lives; a remote directory when running over SSH
Program, Input, MPI processesthe command line is assembled from these and shown below, editable
GPUNone, Any, NVIDIA or AMD; the panel finds the GPUs on the host and sets the solver’s flags (LAMMPS GPU package, CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES / HIP_VISIBLE_DEVICES)
Environmentextra VAR=value lines (one per line)
Sweepa variable name and a list of values: one job per value, {var} in the command replaced, each in its own folder
Result filespatterns of the files to open when the run ends (open when done)

Run starts the job. Jobs appear in the Jobs list with their state, a live log tail, and buttons to cancel, open the results or the folder, and resubmit. Files a running solver writes can be opened while it runs; auraFX reloads them as they change (live reload).

Simulation cases from the pipeline

Six templates write their input files from the data selected in the pipeline, then run the solver:

TemplateSelected dataFiles written into the working directory
MOOSE polycrystala grain mesh (from Assign Grains, or Overlay Mesh of a microstructure)mesh.e (one block per grain, block id = grain id), grain_euler.txt (each block’s Euler angles, read with PropertyReadFile), grains.tex (the same orientations for EulerAngleFileReader), slip_systems.txt, polycrystal.i
SPPARKS grain growtha microstructure volume with FeatureIdsgrains.spparks (the site file), in.potts (a Potts-model run that reads it)
PINNACLE crystal plasticitya grain meshmesh.e, orientations_points.csv (each element’s centre with its grain and Euler angles), case.i
DAMASK crystal plasticity (grid)a microstructure volume with FeatureIds and orientationsgrid.vti (cell array material = grain id − 1), material.yaml (the phase from the material table, then one material per grain with its mean orientation as a quaternion), load.yaml (a strain rate along the axis, the other normal stresses free)
FFT texture filea microstructure volumemicro.txt: one line per voxel, x fastest: φ1 Φ φ2 (Bunge, degrees), x y z (voxel indices from 1), grain id, phase id; micro_README.txt with the grid size
LAMMPS polycrystala microstructure volumeatoms.data (every grain filled with the material’s FCC or BCC lattice turned to the grain’s orientation; of two atoms closer than half the nearest-neighbour distance across a boundary one is removed; periodic box), atom_grains.txt and atoms.dump (each atom’s grain), in.lammps (relax, then heat and hold)

Choose the template, select the data in the pipeline (the panel shows it under Case from), and set the case:

  • Material: the elastic constants, shear modulus and Burgers vector come from the material table of Crystal Mechanics. Only cubic materials (FCC, BCC) are offered.
  • Pull along, strain and steps: the faces named x- and x+ (or y-/y+, z-/z+) are held and pulled. The faces of the other two directions are held on their lower side when the mesh names them. Box Mesh, Plate Mesh, Dog-bone Mesh and the Geometry source name their faces this way.
  • For SPPARKS: Monte Carlo steps and temperature.
  • For LAMMPS: the lattice constant comes from the material’s Burgers vector (a = b√2 for FCC, 2b/√3 for BCC; 4.05 Å for aluminium), and the volume’s longest side holds about 40 lattice cells unless angstrom_per_unit says how many ångström one unit of the volume is. The potential file named in in.lammps (Al.eam.alloy and so on) is yours to provide.

A DAMASK case written this way was checked with DAMASK’s own Python package: it loads the grid and the load case, finds the material file complete and valid, and its own conversion of the grains’ Euler angles gives the same quaternions.

Run writes the files and starts the job. For a remote machine they are written to a local folder and uploaded to the working directory there. With open when done, the results open when the run ends, named after the run:

  • MOOSE: polycrystal_out.e, with one block per grain.
  • SPPARKS: dump.potts, each snapshot a volume with FeatureIds.
  • PINNACLE: solver/fields.pvd, the field snapshots as a time series.
  • DAMASK: grid_load.hdf5 (DAMASK’s result file; post-process it with DAMASK’s tools).
  • LAMMPS: dump.lammpstrj, the atoms over time.

The input files are a starting point. Edit them for the material’s hardening and slip parameters, the boundary conditions and the solver settings before production runs.

poly = afx.plate_mesh().assign_grains(grains=30, blocks=True)
case = afx.simulation_case(poly, "moose", "~/runs/plate", material="Copper", axis="x",
                           strain=0.02, steps=50)        # only write the files
print(case["command"], case["files"])
jid = afx.run_case(poly, "pinnacle", "/data/runs/plate", strain=0.02, steps=40, wait=True)
jid = afx.run_case(volume, "spparks", "~/gg", host="cluster", np=16, mc_steps=500)
v = afx.voronoi_grains(cells=[64, 64, 64], grains=200, periodic=True)
afx.simulation_case(v, "damask", "~/runs/damask", material="Copper", strain=0.05, steps=100)
afx.simulation_case(v, "fft", "~/runs/fft")
afx.simulation_case(v, "lammps", "~/runs/md", material="Aluminium", angstrom_per_unit=5)

run_case takes host, exe (the solver program), np, slurm, auto_load and wait as afx.submit does, plus the case options: material, axis, strain, steps, end_time, fixed and loaded (other set names), mc_steps, temperature, dump_every, seed, and for LAMMPS lattice_constant (Å) and angstrom_per_unit.

Grains on the results. MOOSE keeps the grain blocks. A PINNACLE result has no grain array, but every element sits on its own point of orientations_points.csv, so Assign Grains (nearest point) gives the grains back exactly. Statistics per grain then come from the arrays:

res = afx.load("/data/runs/plate/solver/fields.pvd")
g = res.assign_grains(afx.load("/data/runs/plate/orientations_points.csv"), method="Nearest point")
import numpy as np
gid, sxx = g.array("grain_id", "cells"), g.array("stress", "cells")[:, 0]
for k in np.unique(gid):
    print(k, sxx[gid == k].mean())

Limits:

  • Every element and site needs a grain: no grain id 0. Fill Bad Data fills unindexed cells of a volume.
  • The crystal-plasticity cases cover cubic crystals (FCC and BCC).
  • SPPARKS sites lie on a simple-cubic lattice of the volume’s cells. The lattice constant is the cell size when the cells are cubes, else 1.

Remote machines

Remote machines (SSH) holds the hosts: a name, the SSH target (user@machine, or an alias from ~/.ssh/config), port, key file, jump host, and setup lines run before every command (module loads, source of an environment, AURAFX_SERVER=...). SSH keys or an agent must be set up so that ssh host works without a password prompt. The same hosts serve File › Open on Server… (see Data on another machine).

GPUs lists the GPUs of each host; Browse files opens a remote file browser to pick inputs or results.

From Python

jid = afx.submit("lmp -sf gpu -pk gpu 1 -in in.melt", host="rocm-server",
                 workdir="~/runs/melt", gpu="amd:0", outputs="dump.melt", auto_load=True)
afx.sweep("lmp -var T {T} -in in.melt", T=[300, 600, 900], host="cluster", workdir="~/sweep")
afx.jobs(); afx.job(jid); afx.cancel(jid)
afx.gpus("rocm-server"); afx.hosts()
afx.load_remote("rocm-server", "~/runs/melt/dump.melt")   # copy a file here and open it

gpu is None, "any", "nvidia", "amd" or e.g. "nvidia:0,1". afx.job(id) returns the state, exit code, log tail and output files.

Data on another machine

When the data lives on a cluster or a GPU box, auraFX can read and filter it there and bring back only what you look at, with nothing to set up beyond SSH.

How it works

aurafx-server is a small program without a window. The app starts it on the host over SSH (ssh host aurafx-server --stdio) and talks to it through that connection, so keys, jump hosts and ~/.ssh/config work as usual and no port is opened. Files you open on the server are read there; filters you add below them (Slice, Clip, Contour, Threshold, Stream Tracer, temporal filters and so on) run there too. What comes back:

SituationWhat is transferred
Visible, output smaller than 64 MBthe whole output
Visible, largerits outer surface (all arrays), which is what the 3D view draws
Hidden (for example a reader with a slice below it)only its summary: arrays, ranges, bounds, time steps
Bring all data ticked in Properties, or a filter below it that runs herethe whole output

The Information panel always describes the full data on the server. Python filters (Programmable Filter, Python Calculator) and plugin filters run on this computer, on the data brought over. The server keeps results, so changing one filter only re-runs that filter there.

Setting up a host

Everything is in one window: Remote › Open data on another machine… (also File › Open on Server…). It walks through four steps:

  1. The machine. Pick it, or open Add a machine and type its SSH target (user@machine or an alias from ~/.ssh/config; the aliases found there are offered as buttons), with a port, key file or jump host if needed. ssh machine must work without a password prompt. Machines added here are also the Simulations panel’s, and the other way round. “This computer” runs the server locally, which is handy for trying it out.
  2. aurafx-server on it. Check server shows its version and GPU; Install server copies the one shipped with the app to ~/.local/bin (Linux hosts of the same architecture; the packages run on glibc 2.35 or newer). You can also build it there with cargo build --release -p aurafx-server, or point AURAFX_SERVER at it in the host’s setup lines.
  3. Where the picture is drawn. Auto brings the geometry here unless even its surface is too big to send; On the server renders it with the server’s GPU and sends only pictures (the 3D view on this computer shows them, merged with local objects by depth; picking does not work on them); On this computer always brings the geometry. Each object keeps the choice, and Properties › Display › Draw on changes it later.
  4. Open a file. Browse to it and double-click, or select it and press Open on ….

Opening files

A cloud icon marks objects that run on a server; hover it, or look at the top of Properties, to see what was transferred and how long the server took. From Python:

d = afx.load("/scratch/run42/out.e", host="cluster")   # a Simulations host, ssh alias or user@machine
d = afx.load("ssh://cluster/scratch/run42/out.e")       # the same
s = d.slice(normal=(0, 0, 1))                            # runs on the server
s.set(all_data=True)                                     # bring the whole output over
afx.servers()                                            # connections and the machines' details

Reader options on the server

Files on a server have the same Reader options as local ones (an MPAS file’s vertical level and layout, a NetCDF file’s grid, a CAM-SE file’s connectivity file…). The server looks at the file and reports its options with its first answer, so they appear under Properties a moment after the file opens. Changing one has the server read the file again with it; on a parallel server, every process reads its piece with it. Paths in options, such as a CAM-SE connectivity file, are paths on the server.

d = afx.load("/scratch/ocean/output.nc", host="cluster")
d.set(vertical_level=10)          # read on the server

From Python, an option the file does not have is refused once the server has said which ones it has; before that, the server ignores names it does not know. Saved states keep the options.

Drawing on the server

Some outputs are too big even as a surface. Then the server draws the object with its own GPU and sends pictures (a color and a depth image per view, compressed), which the 3D view blends by depth with the local objects, so everything hides and is hidden correctly. While you move the camera the last picture stays up until the next one arrives.
